========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I’m here because I can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. Like at work, I’ll be in the middle of an experiment or on a grant call, and then I’ll notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or there’s this weird little peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can think about.

And then hours are gone. I mean, sometimes it feels important, like I’ve found some clue everyone else missed, but meanwhile the actual thing people needed from me is just… not done. Safety meetings are terrible for that too, because there are so many noises and little details in the room, and I’ll start following one thread and lose the whole point. It’s gotten bad enough that things are starting to fall apart.

Yes—restless, definitely. Sitting still feels almost… wrong sometimes, like I’m wasting time I don’t have.

I’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there and I’m rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries—because, you know, someone in Germany is already awake, and then Singapore, and then it becomes this whole chain—and I’ll start three analyses at the same time. Not even one after another, all at once.

It’s like if I sit down quietly, I’m missing the one window where I could fix everything. So yes, I fidget, but it’s more than that. I can’t stay put.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call them rituals. I mean, I do things more than once sometimes, but usually it’s because I got pulled into whether it was done correctly—like in the lab, there are procedures where you really are supposed to check things.

But “rituals” makes me think of, I don’t know, having to tap a doorway or count tiles or something like that. I don’t think I have that. Unless you mean… like going back over an email three times because one sentence feels wrong, but then that’s more because the wording matters, especially when it’s to a collaborator, and then I start thinking about how they’ll interpret it and whether it sounds too blunt. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed repeated actions in that way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.

Like I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? Or like I need to keep thinking about it.

But then again, sometimes you have to just submit the thing because there’s a deadline, and everyone acts like that’s normal, which—sorry, this reminds me of this one collaborator who would send comments at the absolute last second and then disappear for two days. So I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.

confused scheduling, and strained supervision started putting the project and lab safety expectations at risk.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or tasks need to be perfect, or that there will be serious consequences if they are not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “perfect” exactly. I mean, in my work, sometimes a tiny error really does matter—like if a calibration is off, or a label is wrong, or someone assumes a sample was handled one way when it wasn’t. So I do care about precision.\n\nBut serious consequences if it isn’t perfect… I’m not sure. That sounds dramatic, and then again, in a lab, “dramatic” can just mean someone has to redo three days of work because the wrong file got saved. Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds.\n\nI guess I notice when things are imperfect, but I don’t know if it’s this feeling that everything has to be perfect or else something terrible will happen. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're unable to complete a task exactly as you envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.\n\nLike I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? Or like I need to keep thinking about it.\n\nBut then again, sometimes you have to just submit the thing because there’s a deadline, and everyone acts like that’s normal, which—sorry, this reminds me of this one collaborator who would send comments at the absolute last second and then disappear for two days. So I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.

In daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.

I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.

It’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.

And meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.

chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. I mean, yes, I like things to be correct, obviously—if the data are wrong, that’s a problem—but that’s not really what it feels like.

It’s like my attention gets hooked. I’ll be in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’ll follow it for hours, even when the actual experiment or the decision people needed from me is just… falling apart.

And then later I realize I never got back to the main thing. It’s frustrating because in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to chase it right then.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. Like at work, I’ll be in the middle of an experiment or on a grant call, and then I’ll notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or there’s this weird little peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can think about.\n\nAnd then hours are gone. I mean, sometimes it feels important, like I’ve found some clue everyone else missed, but meanwhile the actual thing people needed from me is just… not done. Safety meetings are terrible for that too, because there are so many noises and little details in the room, and I’ll start following one thread and lose the whole point. It’s gotten bad enough that things are starting to fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this tendency to get caught up in tiny details is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s stressful because it’s not just, you know, being curious or detail-oriented. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating funny, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum, and my brain just… leaves. I’m gone chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual experiment, the thing people are waiting on, just doesn’t get done. Then I’m trying to explain why something important fell apart because I got stuck on this tiny thing that felt urgent at the time. It makes me anxious, yes—mostly because I can see it happening and still can’t pull myself back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes—restless, definitely. Sitting still feels almost… wrong sometimes, like I’m wasting time I don’t have.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there and I’m rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries—because, you know, someone in Germany is already awake, and then Singapore, and then it becomes this whole chain—and I’ll start three analyses at the same time. Not even one after another, all at once.\n\nIt’s like if I sit down quietly, I’m missing the one window where I could fix everything. So yes, I fidget, but it’s more than that. I can’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them rituals. I mean, I do things more than once sometimes, but usually it’s because I got pulled into whether it was done correctly—like in the lab, there are procedures where you really are supposed to check things.\n\nBut “rituals” makes me think of, I don’t know, having to tap a doorway or count tiles or something like that. I don’t think I have that. Unless you mean… like going back over an email three times because one sentence feels wrong, but then that’s more because the wording matters, especially when it’s to a collaborator, and then I start thinking about how they’ll interpret it and whether it sounds too blunt. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed repeated actions in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or tasks need to be perfect, or that there will be serious consequences if they are not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “perfect” exactly. I mean, in my work, sometimes a tiny error really does matter—like if a calibration is off, or a label is wrong, or someone assumes a sample was handled one way when it wasn’t. So I do care about precision.\n\nBut serious consequences if it isn’t perfect… I’m not sure. That sounds dramatic, and then again, in a lab, “dramatic” can just mean someone has to redo three days of work because the wrong file got saved. Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds.\n\nI guess I notice when things are imperfect, but I don’t know if it’s this feeling that everything has to be perfect or else something terrible will happen. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're unable to complete a task exactly as you envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.\n\nLike I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? Or like I need to keep thinking about it.\n\nBut then again, sometimes you have to just submit the thing because there’s a deadline, and everyone acts like that’s normal, which—sorry, this reminds me of this one collaborator who would send comments at the absolute last second and then disappear for two days. So I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. I’ll be in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’ll follow it for hours, even when the actual experiment or the decision people needed from me is just… falling apart.\n\nAnd then later I realize I never got back to the main thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, but it doesn’t feel like fear exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I miss this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more immediate than that.

I’ll be in the middle of the actual thing I’m supposed to be doing—the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment—and then some tiny thing pulls me. A pump sounds different, or someone mutters something at the side of the room, or I remember that weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m off after it. Hours can go by.

And the stupid part is, the important thing was probably the meeting or the experiment in front of me, but by then I’ve let that fall apart. So… not exactly afraid of mistakes. More like I can’t not follow the detail once it catches me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. Like at work, I’ll be in the middle of an experiment or on a grant call, and then I’ll notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or there’s this weird little peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can think about.\n\nAnd then hours are gone. I mean, sometimes it feels important, like I’ve found some clue everyone else missed, but meanwhile the actual thing people needed from me is just… not done. Safety meetings are terrible for that too, because there are so many noises and little details in the room, and I’ll start following one thread and lose the whole point. It’s gotten bad enough that things are starting to fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this tendency to get caught up in tiny details is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s stressful because it’s not just, you know, being curious or detail-oriented. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating funny, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum, and my brain just… leaves. I’m gone chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual experiment, the thing people are waiting on, just doesn’t get done. Then I’m trying to explain why something important fell apart because I got stuck on this tiny thing that felt urgent at the time. It makes me anxious, yes—mostly because I can see it happening and still can’t pull myself back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes—restless, definitely. Sitting still feels almost… wrong sometimes, like I’m wasting time I don’t have.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there and I’m rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries—because, you know, someone in Germany is already awake, and then Singapore, and then it becomes this whole chain—and I’ll start three analyses at the same time. Not even one after another, all at once.\n\nIt’s like if I sit down quietly, I’m missing the one window where I could fix everything. So yes, I fidget, but it’s more than that. I can’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them rituals. I mean, I do things more than once sometimes, but usually it’s because I got pulled into whether it was done correctly—like in the lab, there are procedures where you really are supposed to check things.\n\nBut “rituals” makes me think of, I don’t know, having to tap a doorway or count tiles or something like that. I don’t think I have that. Unless you mean… like going back over an email three times because one sentence feels wrong, but then that’s more because the wording matters, especially when it’s to a collaborator, and then I start thinking about how they’ll interpret it and whether it sounds too blunt. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed repeated actions in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or tasks need to be perfect, or that there will be serious consequences if they are not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “perfect” exactly. I mean, in my work, sometimes a tiny error really does matter—like if a calibration is off, or a label is wrong, or someone assumes a sample was handled one way when it wasn’t. So I do care about precision.\n\nBut serious consequences if it isn’t perfect… I’m not sure. That sounds dramatic, and then again, in a lab, “dramatic” can just mean someone has to redo three days of work because the wrong file got saved. Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds.\n\nI guess I notice when things are imperfect, but I don’t know if it’s this feeling that everything has to be perfect or else something terrible will happen. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're unable to complete a task exactly as you envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.\n\nLike I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? Or like I need to keep thinking about it.\n\nBut then again, sometimes you have to just submit the thing because there’s a deadline, and everyone acts like that’s normal, which—sorry, this reminds me of this one collaborator who would send comments at the absolute last second and then disappear for two days. So I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. I mean, yes, I like things to be correct, obviously—if the data are wrong, that’s a problem—but that’s not really what it feels like.\n\nIt’s like my attention gets hooked. I’ll be in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s where I am. Like at work, I’ll be in the middle of an experiment or on a grant call, and then I’ll notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or there’s this weird little peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can think about.\n\nAnd then hours are gone. I mean, sometimes it feels important, like I’ve found some clue everyone else missed, but meanwhile the actual thing people needed from me is just… not done. Safety meetings are terrible for that too, because there are so many noises and little details in the room, and I’ll start following one thread and lose the whole point. It’s gotten bad enough that things are starting to fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this tendency to get caught up in tiny details is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s stressful because it’s not just, you know, being curious or detail-oriented. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating funny, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum, and my brain just… leaves. I’m gone chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual experiment, the thing people are waiting on, just doesn’t get done. Then I’m trying to explain why something important fell apart because I got stuck on this tiny thing that felt urgent at the time. It makes me anxious, yes—mostly because I can see it happening and still can’t pull myself back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes—restless, definitely. Sitting still feels almost… wrong sometimes, like I’m wasting time I don’t have.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there and I’m rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries—because, you know, someone in Germany is already awake, and then Singapore, and then it becomes this whole chain—and I’ll start three analyses at the same time. Not even one after another, all at once.\n\nIt’s like if I sit down quietly, I’m missing the one window where I could fix everything. So yes, I fidget, but it’s more than that. I can’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them rituals. I mean, I do things more than once sometimes, but usually it’s because I got pulled into whether it was done correctly—like in the lab, there are procedures where you really are supposed to check things.\n\nBut “rituals” makes me think of, I don’t know, having to tap a doorway or count tiles or something like that. I don’t think I have that. Unless you mean… like going back over an email three times because one sentence feels wrong, but then that’s more because the wording matters, especially when it’s to a collaborator, and then I start thinking about how they’ll interpret it and whether it sounds too blunt. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed repeated actions in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or tasks need to be perfect, or that there will be serious consequences if they are not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “perfect” exactly. I mean, in my work, sometimes a tiny error really does matter—like if a calibration is off, or a label is wrong, or someone assumes a sample was handled one way when it wasn’t. So I do care about precision.\n\nBut serious consequences if it isn’t perfect… I’m not sure. That sounds dramatic, and then again, in a lab, “dramatic” can just mean someone has to redo three days of work because the wrong file got saved. Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds.\n\nI guess I notice when things are imperfect, but I don’t know if it’s this feeling that everything has to be perfect or else something terrible will happen. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're unable to complete a task exactly as you envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.\n\nLike I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? Or like I need to keep thinking about it.\n\nBut then again, sometimes you have to just submit the thing because there’s a deadline, and everyone acts like that’s normal, which—sorry, this reminds me of this one collaborator who would send comments at the absolute last second and then disappear for two days. So I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. It’s frustrating because in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to chase it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get caught up in details because you're afraid of missing something important or making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but it doesn’t feel like fear exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. It’s frustrating because in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to chase it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get caught up in details because you're afraid of missing something important or making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but it doesn’t feel like fear exactly. A pump sounds different, or someone mutters something at the side of the room, or I remember that weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m off after it. Hours can go by.\n\nAnd the stupid part is, the important thing was probably the meeting or the experiment in front of me, but by then I’ve let that fall apart. So… not exactly afraid of mistakes. More like I can’t not follow the detail once it catches me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is more about making sure everything is correct or about being unable to ignore the details that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more that I can’t ignore them. I mean, I care about being correct, of course, but that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nIt’s like—I’ll be trying to focus on the actual experiment, and then something small catches me. A pump vibration, or some comment a student makes off to the side, or I suddenly remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that for hours.\n\nAnd then the grant call, or the safety meeting, or whatever people actually needed from me just falls apart. That’s why I came in, honestly. I can’t keep my mind on the main thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on the main task if the small details that catch your attention were somehow resolved or eliminated?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but honestly I don’t know. Because it isn’t just one detail to remove, you know? If the pump vibration were fixed, then it would be the student whispering something, or I’d suddenly remember that old spectrum with the strange peak—sorry, that one still bothers me because I never really figured out if it was contamination or the detector acting up.\n\nBut yes, I mean… if somehow none of those things grabbed me, maybe I could stay with the actual experiment. That’s the whole problem. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the main task anymore. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I think sometimes I can, if it’s something small or if no one is going to look too closely at it.

But “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because usually the thing changes while I’m doing it. Like with an experiment, the picture in my head at the start is never the thing at the end, because then there’s some odd reading or someone asks a question, and suddenly the whole shape of it is different. I don’t know if that counts as unfinished or just… not the same task anymore.

So I guess I don’t have a clean answer. I can let some things be incomplete-looking, maybe, but I also lose track of what “complete” was supposed to mean in the first place.

And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. the details that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more that I can’t ignore them. I mean, I care about being correct, of course, but that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nIt’s like—I’ll be trying to focus on the actual experiment, and then something small catches me. A pump vibration, or some comment a student makes off to the side, or I suddenly remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that for hours.\n\nAnd then the grant call, or the safety meeting, or whatever people actually needed from me just falls apart. That’s why I came in, honestly. I can’t keep my mind on the main thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on the main task if the small details that catch your attention were somehow resolved or eliminated?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but honestly I don’t know. Because it isn’t just one detail to remove, you know? If the pump vibration were fixed, then it would be the student whispering something, or I’d suddenly remember that old spectrum with the strange peak—sorry, that one still bothers me because I never really figured out if it was contamination or the detector acting up.\n\nBut yes, I mean… if somehow none of those things grabbed me, maybe I could stay with the actual experiment. That’s the whole problem. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the main task anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting and then one small thing hooks me, and I’m off chasing it for hours while the thing people actually needed from me falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could consider a task complete even if it's not done exactly as you had envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I think sometimes I can, if it’s something small or if no one is going to look too closely at it.\n\nBut “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because usually the thing changes while I’m doing it. Like with an experiment, the picture in my head at the start is never the thing at the end, because then there’s some odd reading or someone asks a question, and suddenly the whole shape of it is different. I don’t know if that counts as unfinished or just… not the same task anymore.\n\nSo I guess I don’t have a clean answer. I can let some things be incomplete-looking, maybe, but I also lose track of what “complete” was supposed to mean in the first place.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're trying to perfect things when you get caught up in details, or is it more that you just can't help but notice them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

It’s more that I can’t help but notice them. I don’t think I’m trying to make things perfect, exactly.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. Like at work, I’ll be in the middle of an experiment or on a grant call, and then I’ll notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or there’s this weird little peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can think about.\n\nAnd then hours are gone. I mean, sometimes it feels important, like I’ve found some clue everyone else missed, but meanwhile the actual thing people needed from me is just… not done. Safety meetings are terrible for that too, because there are so many noises and little details in the room, and I’ll start following one thread and lose the whole point. It’s gotten bad enough that things are starting to fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this tendency to get caught up in tiny details is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s stressful because it’s not just, you know, being curious or detail-oriented. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating funny, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum, and my brain just… leaves. I’m gone chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual experiment, the thing people are waiting on, just doesn’t get done. Then I’m trying to explain why something important fell apart because I got stuck on this tiny thing that felt urgent at the time. It makes me anxious, yes—mostly because I can see it happening and still can’t pull myself back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes—restless, definitely. Sitting still feels almost… wrong sometimes, like I’m wasting time I don’t have.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there and I’m rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries—because, you know, someone in Germany is already awake, and then Singapore, and then it becomes this whole chain—and I’ll start three analyses at the same time. Not even one after another, all at once.\n\nIt’s like if I sit down quietly, I’m missing the one window where I could fix everything. So yes, I fidget, but it’s more than that. I can’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them rituals. I mean, I do things more than once sometimes, but usually it’s because I got pulled into whether it was done correctly—like in the lab, there are procedures where you really are supposed to check things.\n\nBut “rituals” makes me think of, I don’t know, having to tap a doorway or count tiles or something like that. I don’t think I have that. Unless you mean… like going back over an email three times because one sentence feels wrong, but then that’s more because the wording matters, especially when it’s to a collaborator, and then I start thinking about how they’ll interpret it and whether it sounds too blunt. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed repeated actions in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or tasks need to be perfect, or that there will be serious consequences if they are not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “perfect” exactly. I mean, in my work, sometimes a tiny error really does matter—like if a calibration is off, or a label is wrong, or someone assumes a sample was handled one way when it wasn’t. So I do care about precision.\n\nBut serious consequences if it isn’t perfect… I’m not sure. That sounds dramatic, and then again, in a lab, “dramatic” can just mean someone has to redo three days of work because the wrong file got saved. Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds.\n\nI guess I notice when things are imperfect, but I don’t know if it’s this feeling that everything has to be perfect or else something terrible will happen. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're unable to complete a task exactly as you envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.\n\nLike I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? Or like I need to keep thinking about it.\n\nBut then again, sometimes you have to just submit the thing because there’s a deadline, and everyone acts like that’s normal, which—sorry, this reminds me of this one collaborator who would send comments at the absolute last second and then disappear for two days. So I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. I mean, yes, I like things to be correct, obviously—if the data are wrong, that’s a problem—but that’s not really what it feels like.\n\nIt’s like my attention gets hooked. I’ll be in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’ll follow it for hours, even when the actual experiment or the decision people needed from me is just… falling apart.\n\nAnd then later I realize I never got back to the main thing. It’s frustrating because in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to chase it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get caught up in details because you're afraid of missing something important or making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but it doesn’t feel like fear exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I miss this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more immediate than that.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of the actual thing I’m supposed to be doing—the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment—and then some tiny thing pulls me. A pump sounds different, or someone mutters something at the side of the room, or I remember that weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m off after it. Hours can go by.\n\nAnd the stupid part is, the important thing was probably the meeting or the experiment in front of me, but by then I’ve let that fall apart. So… not exactly afraid of mistakes. More like I can’t not follow the detail once it catches me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is more about making sure everything is correct or about being unable to ignore the details that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more that I can’t ignore them. I mean, I care about being correct, of course, but that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nIt’s like—I’ll be trying to focus on the actual experiment, and then something small catches me. A pump vibration, or some comment a student makes off to the side, or I suddenly remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that for hours.\n\nAnd then the grant call, or the safety meeting, or whatever people actually needed from me just falls apart. That’s why I came in, honestly. I can’t keep my mind on the main thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on the main task if the small details that catch your attention were somehow resolved or eliminated?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but honestly I don’t know. Because it isn’t just one detail to remove, you know? If the pump vibration were fixed, then it would be the student whispering something, or I’d suddenly remember that old spectrum with the strange peak—sorry, that one still bothers me because I never really figured out if it was contamination or the detector acting up.\n\nBut yes, I mean… if somehow none of those things grabbed me, maybe I could stay with the actual experiment. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. I mean, yes, I like things to be correct, obviously—if the data are wrong, that’s a problem—but that’s not really what it feels like.\n\nIt’s like my attention gets hooked. I’ll be in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’ll follow it for hours, even when the actual experiment or the decision people needed from me is just… falling apart.\n\nAnd then later I realize I never got back to the main thing. It’s frustrating because in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to chase it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get caught up in details because you're afraid of missing something important or making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but it doesn’t feel like fear exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I miss this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more immediate than that.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of the actual thing I’m supposed to be doing—the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment—and then some tiny thing pulls me. A pump sounds different, or someone mutters something at the side of the room, or I remember that weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m off after it. Hours can go by.\n\nAnd the stupid part is, the important thing was probably the meeting or the experiment in front of me, but by then I’ve let that fall apart. So… not exactly afraid of mistakes. More like I can’t not follow the detail once it catches me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is more about making sure everything is correct or about being unable to ignore the details that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more that I can’t ignore them. I mean, I care about being correct, of course, but that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nIt’s like—I’ll be trying to focus on the actual experiment, and then something small catches me. A pump vibration, or some comment a student makes off to the side, or I suddenly remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that for hours.\n\nAnd then the grant call, or the safety meeting, or whatever people actually needed from me just falls apart. That’s why I came in, honestly. I can’t keep my mind on the main thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on the main task if the small details that catch your attention were somehow resolved or eliminated?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but honestly I don’t know. Because it isn’t just one detail to remove, you know? If the pump vibration were fixed, then it would be the student whispering something, or I’d suddenly remember that old spectrum with the strange peak—sorry, that one still bothers me because I never really figured out if it was contamination or the detector acting up.\n\nBut yes, I mean… if somehow none of those things grabbed me, maybe I could stay with the actual experiment. That’s the whole problem. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the main task anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting and then one small thing hooks me, and I’m off chasing it for hours while the thing people actually needed from me falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could consider a task complete even if it's not done exactly as you had envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I think sometimes I can, if it’s something small or if no one is going to look too closely at it.\n\nBut “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because usually the thing changes while I’m doing it. Like with an experiment, the picture in my head at the start is never the thing at the end, because then there’s some odd reading or someone asks a question, and suddenly the whole shape of it is different. I don’t know if that counts as unfinished or just… not the same task anymore.\n\nSo I guess I don’t have a clean answer. I can let some things be incomplete-looking, maybe, but I also lose track of what “complete” was supposed to mean in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to perfect things when you get caught up in details, or is it more that you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help but notice them. I don’t think I’m trying to make things perfect, exactly.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of something I’m supposed to stay with—like the actual experiment, or a grant call, or a safety meeting—and then there’s this pump vibration, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some weird peak from an old spectrum. And then my brain just… goes there. I start chasing it, and hours can disappear.\n\nMeanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me is just falling apart. That’s why I came in, really. I can’t keep my mind on the main thing anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it starts because it might be important, at least that’s what my brain tells me. Like, a pump vibration could mean something’s wrong with the setup, or a student’s comment might actually matter, or that odd peak in an old spectrum—those things can be real clues.

But then it doesn’t stop there. Once I notice it, I can’t let it go. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. Like at work, I’ll be in the middle of an experiment or on a grant call, and then I’ll notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or there’s this weird little peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can think about.\n\nAnd then hours are gone. I mean, I do things more than once sometimes, but usually it’s because I got pulled into whether it was done correctly—like in the lab, there are procedures where you really are supposed to check things.\n\nBut “rituals” makes me think of, I don’t know, having to tap a doorway or count tiles or something like that. I don’t think I have that. Unless you mean… like going back over an email three times because one sentence feels wrong, but then that’s more because the wording matters, especially when it’s to a collaborator, and then I start thinking about how they’ll interpret it and whether it sounds too blunt. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed repeated actions in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or tasks need to be perfect, or that there will be serious consequences if they are not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “perfect” exactly. I mean, in my work, sometimes a tiny error really does matter—like if a calibration is off, or a label is wrong, or someone assumes a sample was handled one way when it wasn’t. So I do care about precision.\n\nBut serious consequences if it isn’t perfect… I’m not sure. That sounds dramatic, and then again, in a lab, “dramatic” can just mean someone has to redo three days of work because the wrong file got saved. Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds.\n\nI guess I notice when things are imperfect, but I don’t know if it’s this feeling that everything has to be perfect or else something terrible will happen. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're unable to complete a task exactly as you envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “as I envisioned it” is tricky, because half the time the way I envisioned it changes while I’m doing it.\n\nLike I’ll start with one plan for a report or an experiment setup, and then I’ll notice something else that could be improved, and then suddenly the original task isn’t even the same task anymore. So if I can’t finish it exactly the way I pictured it at first, I don’t know that I feel anxious, exactly. Maybe irritated? I haven’t really noticed a specific uneasy feeling tied to it not matching my original vision.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to control or organize your environment, even when it's not necessarily required?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, not control like everything has to be color-coded at home or anything. But at work, if things feel loose, I have this almost physical need to get in there and straighten it out.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is even there, and I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries because they’re awake when we’re not, and then I’ll open three new analyses because suddenly it seems obvious how to fix all of it. And it’s not always required, exactly. It’s more like… if I don’t do it right then, I’m wasting the chance to get everything lined up.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds more intense than “organizing my environment,” but yes, I guess I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to make things symmetrical or exact in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical or exact… not really in the sense of, like, the mug has to be lined up with the edge of the table or I can’t leave the room.\n\nI mean, I notice if something is off-center, especially on a graph or a slide, because then it looks sloppy and people start commenting on the wrong thing instead of the data. Actually, that happened once in a presentation where someone spent ten minutes talking about axis labels instead of the result, which was maddening because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIn daily life, I don’t think I have a strong urge for symmetry. Exactness, maybe in work things, but not like arranging objects or needing things even. I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is because you want everything to be just right, or because you have trouble ignoring small things that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one. I don’t know that it’s about wanting everything to be “just right” in some tidy, perfect way.\n\nIt’s more like small things grab me and then I can’t let them go. That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice the vibration in a pump, or one comment a student makes off to the side, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then suddenly that’s the whole thing. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the report, the meeting decision, whatever—just kind of falls apart. I don’t mean to do it. It just feels like my attention gets pulled sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting caught up in details because you're trying to make sure everything is correct, or because you just can't help but notice them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t help noticing them. I mean, yes, I like things to be correct, obviously—if the data are wrong, that’s a problem—but that’s not really what it feels like.\n\nIt’s like my attention gets hooked. I’ll be in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’ll follow it for hours, even when the actual experiment or the decision people needed from me is just… falling apart.\n\nAnd then later I realize I never got back to the main thing. It’s frustrating because in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to chase it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get caught up in details because you're afraid of missing something important or making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but it doesn’t feel like fear exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I miss this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more immediate than that.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of the actual thing I’m supposed to be doing—the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment—and then some tiny thing pulls me. A pump sounds different, or someone mutters something at the side of the room, or I remember that weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m off after it. Hours can go by.\n\nAnd the stupid part is, the important thing was probably the meeting or the experiment in front of me, but by then I’ve let that fall apart. So… not exactly afraid of mistakes. More like I can’t not follow the detail once it catches me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to get caught up in details is more about making sure everything is correct or about being unable to ignore the details that catch your attention?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more that I can’t ignore them. I mean, I care about being correct, of course, but that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nIt’s like—I’ll be trying to focus on the actual experiment, and then something small catches me. A pump vibration, or some comment a student makes off to the side, or I suddenly remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that for hours.\n\nAnd then the grant call, or the safety meeting, or whatever people actually needed from me just falls apart. That’s why I came in, honestly. I can’t keep my mind on the main thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on the main task if the small details that catch your attention were somehow resolved or eliminated?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but honestly I don’t know. That’s the whole problem. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the main task anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting and then one small thing hooks me, and I’m off chasing it for hours while the thing people actually needed from me falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could consider a task complete even if it's not done exactly as you had envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ll be on a grant call or sitting in a safety meeting, and suddenly I’m chasing one of those little things for hours, and meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me is falling apart.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're getting caught up in these details because you're trying to achieve a perfect outcome, or because you're having trouble ignoring the distractions?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Manic_Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore—during a grant call or safety meeting I'll notice a pump vibration, a student's side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum and suddenly I'm off chasing that for hours while the thing everyone needs from me falls apart.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not really perfection, no. I mean, I like things to be right, obviously, but that’s not what it feels like.

It’s more like I can’t ignore something once it catches. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating a little strangely, or a student will say something off to the side, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m gone. I’m chasing that thread for hours.

And then the thing everyone actually needed from me is just… falling apart while I’m off with the side thing. So no, I don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect. I think I’m just getting pulled away and I can’t make myself come back.
